There might be a king’s border town with strong ramparts, an arched gateway, and only one door, and there, if a man were wishing to depart, how would he do so?” “He would depart through the door, venerable sir.” “Then, if there was another man wishing to depart, how would he do so?” “He would depart in the same way as the first man, venerable sir.”

“Does the first man, your majesty, instruct the later man, ‘where I go, you should go,’ or does the later man instruct the first man ‘where you will go, there I will go’?” “Certainly not, venerable sir, there is no conversation between them, they go because of the door.” “In the same way, your majesty, because of the door eye consciousness arises, then mind consciousness arises. And not because eye consciousness instructs mind consciousness, ‘where I arise, you should arise,’ nor does mind consciousness instruct eye consciousness ‘where you will arise, there I will arise’. There is no conversation between them, they arise because of the door.”

“How is it, Venerable Nāgasena, that because of habit mind consciousness arises where eye consciousness arises? Please give me an analogy.” “What do you think, your majesty? If one cart goes first, then which way would a second cart go?” “It would go in the same way as the first cart, venerable sir.”

“Does the first cart, your majesty, instruct the later cart, ‘where I go, you should go,’ or does the later cart instruct the first cart ‘where you will go, there I will go’?” “Certainly not, venerable sir, there is no conversation between them, they go because of habit.” “In the same way, your majesty, because of habit eye consciousness arises, then mind consciousness arises. And not because eye consciousness instructs mind consciousness, ‘where I arise, you should arise,’ nor does mind consciousness instruct eye consciousness ‘where you will arise, there I will arise’. There is no conversation between them, they arise because of habit.”

“How is it, Venerable Nāgasena, that because of practice mind consciousness arises where eye consciousness arises? Please give me an analogy.” “Just as, your majesty, a beginner might be clumsy in the arts of hand reckoning, calculation, mental reckoning, and writing, but then, after some time, with careful working and practice, he would become skilful. In the same way, your majesty, because of practice eye consciousness arises, then mind consciousness arises. And not because eye consciousness instructs mind consciousness, ‘where I arise, you should arise,’ nor does mind consciousness instruct eye consciousness ‘where you will arise, there I will arise’. There is no conversation between them, they arise because of practice.”

“Venerable Nāgasena, does mind consciousness arise where ear consciousness arises? ... where nose consciousness arises … where tongue consciousness arises … where body consciousness arises?” “Yes, your majesty, mind consciousness arises where body consciousness arises.”

“Venerable Nāgasena, does body consciousness arise first, then mind consciousness after, or does mind consciousness arise first, then body consciousness after?” “Body consciousness arises first, your majesty, then mind consciousness arises after.”

“Venerable Nāgasena, does … there is no conversation between them, they arise because of practice.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Eye Consciousness, Etc. seventh

## mil3.3.8

8. Distinguishing Characteristic of Contact Question

### Milinda’s Questions

### Examination Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, do contact and also feeling arise where mind consciousness arises?” “Yes, your majesty, where mind consciousness arises, there too contact, feeling, perception, intention, applied thought, and examination arise, and all mental states that arise there are led by contact.”

“Venerable Nāgasena, what is the distinguishing characteristic of contact?” “Touching, your majesty, is the distinguishing characteristic of contact.”

“Please give me an analogy.” “Suppose, your majesty, that two rams were fighting. One of these rams is to be seen as the eye, the other ram is to be seen as form, and the meeting of the two is to be seen as contact.”

“Give me another analogy.” “Suppose, your majesty, that two hands were clapped together. One of these hands is to be seen as the eye, the other hand is to be seen as form, and the meeting of the two is to be seen as contact.”

“Give me another analogy.” “Suppose, your majesty, that two cymbals were clanged together. One of these cymbals is to be seen as the eye, the other cymbal is to be seen as form, and the meeting of the two is to be seen as contact.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Distinguishing Characteristic of Contact Question eighth

## mil3.3.9

9. Distinguishing Characteristic of Feeling Question

### Milinda’s Questions

### Examination Chapter

“Venerable Nāgasena, what is the distinguishing characteristic of feeling?” “Being felt and experiencing, your majesty, are the distinguishing characteristics of feeling.”

“Please give me an analogy.” “Just as, your majesty, some person or other might perform a service for the king, and the king, satisfied, would repay that service. And that person, because of that service, would go about provided with and possessed of the five strands of sensual pleasure, and it might occur to him ‘previously I performed a service for the king and he, satisfied, repaid me for that service, and for that reason I experience such and such feelings.’

Or just as, your majesty, some person or other might perform a good deed and at the beak up of the body after dying, might be reborn in a good destination, in a heavenly world, and there would go about provided with and possessed of the five strands of divine sensual pleasure, and it might occur to him ‘previously I performed a good deed, and for that reason I experience such and such feelings.’ In the same way, your majesty, being felt and experiencing are the distinguishing characteristics of feeling.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Distinguishing Characteristic of Feeling Question ninth

## mil3.4.1

1. Question on Analysing Contact, Etc.

### Milinda’s Questions

### Nibbāna Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, is it possible, having repeatedly analysed these mental states that are of the nature to arise together, to declare a difference between them, saying ‘this is contact, this is feeling, this is perception, this is intention, this is consciousness, this is applied thought, this is examination’?” “No, your majesty, it is not possible to do that.”

“Please give me an analogy.” “Just as if, your majesty, a king’s cook might prepare a soup or sauce and put in it curds, salt, ginger, cumin, hot pepper, and various other things, and the king might say ‘bring me a taste of curds, a taste of salt, a taste of ginger, a taste of cumin, a taste of hot pepper, or a taste of all the other things put in.’ Is it possible, your majesty, having repeatedly analysed these tastes that are of the nature to arise together to bring forward an acidic flavour, a salty flavour, a bitter flavour, a sharp flavour, or a pungent flavour, or a sweet flavour?” “It is certainly not possible, venerable sir, to do that.” “In the same way, your majesty, it is not possible, having repeatedly analysed these mental states that are of the nature to arise together, to declare a difference between them, saying ‘this is contact, this is feeling, this is perception, this is intention, this is consciousness, this is applied thought, this is examination’?”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Analysing Contact, Etc. first

## mil3.4.10

10. Question on Knowing the Happiness of Nibbāna

### Milinda’s Questions

### Nibbāna Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, does one who does not attain nibbāna know the happiness of nibbāna?” “Yes, your majesty, one who does not attain nibbāna knows the happiness of nibbāna.” “How, Venerable Nāgasena, not attaining it does one know the happiness of nibbāna?” “What do you think, your majesty, would those who haven’t had their hands and feet cut off know the suffering of having one’s hands and feet cut off?” “Yes, venerable sir, they would know.” “How would they know?” “Having heard, venerable sir, the sounds of lamentation of those who have had their hands and feet cut off, they would know the suffering of having one’s hands and feet cut off.” “In the same way, your majesty, having heard the sound of one who has seen nibbāna, one knows the happiness of nibbāna.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Knowing the Happiness of Nibbāna tenth

Nibbāna Chapter fourth

In this chapter ten questions

## mil3.4.2

2. Nāgasena’s Question

### Milinda’s Questions

### Nibbāna Chapter

The elder asked: “Is salt, your majesty, cognizable by the eye?” “Yes, venerable sir, it is cognizable by the eye.” “Think carefully, your majesty.” “Is it then, venerable sir, cognizable by the tongue?” “Yes, your majesty, it is cognizable by the tongue.” “Then, venerable sir, is all salt cognizable by the tongue?” “Yes, your majesty, all salt is cognizable by the tongue.”

“If, venerable sir, all salt is cognizable by the tongue, why then do oxen bring it by the cartload, shouldn’t just salt be brought?” “It is not possible to bring just salt, your majesty, since these things arise together with a diversity in their range, and as well, salt has heaviness. Is it possible to weigh salt on scales?” “Yes, venerable sir, it is possible.” “It is not possible, your majesty, to weigh salt on scales. One weighs the heaviness on scales.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Nāgasena’s Question second

## mil3.4.3

3. Question on the Five Sense Bases Produced from Kamma

### Milinda’s Questions

### Nibbāna Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, are these five sense bases produced from different kamma or from just one kamma?” “They are produced from different kamma, your majesty, not from just one kamma.”

“Please give me an analogy.” “What do you think, your majesty, if one were to sow different seeds in this field, would a diversity of fruits be produced from this diversity of seeds?” “Yes, venerable sir, they would be.” “In the same way, your majesty, these five sense bases are produced from different kamma, and not from just one kamma.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Five Sense Bases Produced from Kamma third

## mil3.4.4

4. Question on the Workings of Different Kamma

### Milinda’s Questions

### Nibbāna Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, why is it that not all people are the same? Some people are short-lived, others long-lived, some have much affliction and others little affliction, some are ugly and others beautiful, some have little power and others great power, some have little wealth and others great wealth, some come from an inferior clan and others from a superior clan, some are foolish and others are wise.”

The elder said: “Why then, your majesty, is it that not all trees are the same? Some are acidic, some are salty, some are bitter, some are pungent, some are astringent, and some are sweet?” “I think, venerable sir, that it is because of the difference in seeds.” “In the same way, your majesty, it is because of the difference in kamma that not all people are the same. Some people are short-lived, others long-lived, some have much affliction and others little affliction, some are ugly and others beautiful, some have little power and others great power, some have little wealth and others great wealth, some come from an inferior clan and others from a superior clan, some are foolish and others are wise. And this too was said, your majesty, by the Blessed One: ‘Young man, beings are the owners of their kamma, heirs to their kamma, born from their kamma, bound to their kamma, and protected by their kamma. Kamma divides beings into inferior and superior.’”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Workings of Different Kamma fourth

## mil3.4.5

5. Question on the Reason for Effort

### Milinda’s Questions

### Nibbāna Chapter

The king said: “Venerable Nāgasena, you told me that, so that this suffering would cease and another suffering would not arise, that was the goal of your going forth. Is it just because of previous effort? Should not one make an effort at the current time?” The elder said: “Effort at the current time is not so useful, effort made previously is useful.”

“Please give me an analogy.” “What do you think, your majesty, when you become thirsty, would you then have a well or a dam dug and say ‘I will drink water’?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “In the same way, effort at the current time is not so useful, effort made previously is useful.”

“Give me another analogy.” “What do you think, your majesty, when you become hungry, would you then have a field ploughed, plant rice, and have the grain brought in, and say ‘I will eat rice’?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “In the same way, effort at the current time is not so useful, effort made previously is useful.”

“Give me another analogy.” “What do you think, your majesty, when you are faced with a battle, would you then have a moat dug, a rampart, a gateway, and a watchtower constructed, and then train in the use of elephants, horses, chariots, bows and swords?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “In the same way, effort at the current time is not so useful, effort made previously is useful. And this too was said, your majesty, by the Blessed One:

‘One should do formerly what one knows is for one’s benefit; Not having thoughts like a wagon driver, one is steadfast in wisdom.

As a wagon driver who has abandoned the well-prepared, even, high road, Climbing on the uneven path, just broods with his axle broken,

Straying from the Dhamma thus, and following what isn’t Dhamma, The lazy one coming face-to-face with death, just broods with his axle broken.’”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Reason for Effort fifth

## mil3.4.6

6. Question on the Nature of Heat in the Fires of Hell

### Milinda’s Questions

### Nibbāna Chapter

The king said: “Venerable Nāgasena, you say that the heat of hell has a far greater intensity than that of a normal fire, and that a small stone placed in a normal fire cooking all day would not be dissolved, whereas a stone the size of a gabled house placed in the fire of hell would be dissolved in a moment. I do not believe this assertion. And then you say that those various beings who have arisen in hell, cooking in that hell for thousands of years are not dissolved. That assertion too I do not believe.”

The elder asked, “What do you think, your majesty, do those female sharks, crocodiles, turtles, pea-hens, and pigeons eat hard stones and gravel?” “Yes, venerable sir, they eat them.” “But then when those have gone down into their abdomens and stomachs are they dissolved?” “Yes, venerable sir, they are dissolved.” “But then is an embryo in their abdomens dissolved?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “For what reason?” “I think, venerable sir, due to the workings of kamma it would not dissolve.” “In the same way, your majesty, those various beings in hell, cooking in that hell for thousands of years are not dissolved. And this too was said, your majesty, by the Blessed One: ‘One does not complete one’s time until there is an end to one’s evil kamma.’”

“Give me another analogy.” “What do you think, your majesty, do female lions, tigers, panthers, and dogs eat hard bones and flesh?” “Yes, venerable sir, they eat them.” “But then when those have gone down into their abdomens and stomachs are they dissolved?” “Yes, venerable sir, they are dissolved.” “But then is an embryo in their abdomens dissolved?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “For what reason?” “I think, venerable sir, due to the workings of kamma it would not dissolve.” “In the same way, your majesty, those various beings in hell, cooking in that hell for thousands of years are not dissolved.”

“Give me another analogy.” “What do you think, your majesty, do those delicate Bactrian Greek women, noble women, brahmin women, and householder women eat hard cakes and meat?” “Yes, venerable sir, they eat them.” “But then when those have gone down into their abdomens and stomachs are they dissolved?” “Yes, venerable sir, they are dissolved.” “But then is an embryo in their abdomens dissolved?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “For what reason?” “I think, venerable sir, due to the workings of kamma it would not dissolve.” “In the same way, your majesty, those various beings in hell, cooking in that hell for thousands of years are not dissolved. And this too was said, your majesty, by the Blessed One: ‘One does not complete one’s time until there is an end to one’s evil kamma.’”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Nature of Heat in the Fires of Hell sixth

## mil3.4.7

7. Question on the Bearing Up of the Earth

### Milinda’s Questions

### Nibbāna Chapter

The king said: “Venerable Nāgasena, you say this great earth is established on water, water is established on air, and air is established on space. I do not believe this assertion.” The elder took some water in a water vessel and explained to King Milinda, “Just as, your majesty, this water is supported by the air, so too is that water supported by air.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Bearing Up of the Earth seventh

## mil3.4.8

8. Question on Cessation and Nibbāna

### Milinda’s Questions

### Nibbāna Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, is cessation nibbāna?” “Yes, your majesty, cessation is nibbāna.” “How, Venerable Nāgasena, is cessation nibbāna?” “All those foolish ordinary people, your majesty, who delight in the internal and external sense bases, welcome them, remain clinging to them, and are carried away by that stream, are not free from birth, old age, death, sorrow, lamentation, suffering, anguish, and despair, they are not free from suffering, I say. A well-taught noble disciple, your majesty, does not delight in the internal and external sense bases, nor welcome them, nor remain clinging to them. The one, not delighting in them, not welcoming them, not remaining clinging to them, is free from craving. From the cessation of craving, there is cessation of birth. From the cessation of birth, old age, death, sorrow, lamentation, suffering, grief, and despair cease, and thus there is the cessation of this whole mass of suffering. In this way, your majesty, cessation is nibbāna.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Cessation and Nibbāna eighth

## mil3.4.9

9. Question on the Attaining of Nibbāna

### Milinda’s Questions

### Nibbāna Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, does everyone attain nibbāna?” “Not everyone, your majesty, attains nibbāna. Whoever, practicing rightly, directly knows those things which should be directly known, completely comprehends those things that should be completely comprehended, abandons those things that should be abandoned, develops those things that should be developed, personally realises those things that should be personally realised, that one attains nibbāna.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Attaining of Nibbāna ninth

## mil3.5.1

1. Question on the Existence or Non-existence of the Buddha

### Milinda’s Questions

### Buddha Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, have you seen the Buddha?” “Certainly not, your majesty.” “Then, have your teachers seen the Buddha?” “Certainly not, your majesty.” “Therefore, Venerable Nāgasena, there is no Buddha.” “Well then, your majesty, have you seen the River Ūhā in the Himalayas?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “Then has your father seen the River Ūhā?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “Because of this, your majesty, there is no River Ūhā.” “There is, venerable sir. Even though the River Ūhā has not been seen by me nor by my father, there is a River Ūhā.” “In the same way, your majesty, even though the Blessed One has not been seen by me nor by my teachers, there is a Blessed One.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Existence or Non-existence of the Buddha first

## mil3.5.10

10. Question on Pointing Out the Buddha

### Milinda’s Questions

### Buddha Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, does the Buddha exist?” “Yes, your majesty, the Blessed One exists.” “Is it then possible, venerable sir, to point to the Buddha as being either here or there?” “The Blessed One has passed away, your majesty, in the element of nibbāna without residue remaining, so it is not possible to point to the Blessed One as being either here or there.”

“Please give me an analogy.” “What do you think, your majesty, when the flame of a great burning mass of fire has been extinguished, is it possible to point to that flame and say it remains here or there?” “Certainly not, venerable sir, for that flame has ceased and gone beyond description.” “In the same way, your majesty, the Blessed One has passed away in the element of nibbāna without residue remaining, so it is not possible to point to the Blessed One as being either here or there. However, your majesty, it is possible to point out the Blessed One through the body of Dhamma, for this Dhamma has been taught by the Blessed One.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Pointing Out the Buddha tenth

Buddha Chapter fifth

In this chapter ten questions

## mil3.5.2

2. Question on the Unsurpassed Nature of the Buddha

### Milinda’s Questions

### Buddha Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, is the Buddha unsurpassed?” “Yes, your majesty, the Blessed One is unsurpassed.” “How, Venerable Nāgasena, do you know the Buddha is unsurpassed if you haven’t seen him before?” “What do you think, your majesty, would those who have not seen the great ocean before know that this great ocean is huge, deep, immeasurable, and unfathomable, such that as these five great rivers that flow into it constantly and continually, that is the Gaṅgā, the Yamunā, the Aciravatī, the Sarabhū, and the Mahī, neither the depletion nor filling up of it is discerned?” “Yes, venerable sir, they would know.” “In the same way, your majesty, when I think of the great disciples who have attained final nibbāna, I know the Blessed One is unsurpassed.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Unsurpassed Nature of the Buddha second

## mil3.5.3

3. Question on Knowing the Unsurpassed Nature of the Buddha

### Milinda’s Questions

### Buddha Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, is it possible to know that the Buddha is unsurpassed?” “Yes, your majesty, it is possible to know that the Blessed One is unsurpassed.” “How, Venerable Nāgasena, is it possible to know that the Buddha is unsurpassed?” “In the past, your majesty, an elder named Tissa was a teacher of writing and many years have passed since he passed away. How is it that he is still known?” “By his writing, venerable sir.” “In the same way, your majesty, whoever sees the Dhamma sees the Blessed One, for the Dhamma was taught by the Blessed One.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Knowing the Unsurpassed Nature of the Buddha third

## mil3.5.4

4. Question on Seeing the Dhamma

### Milinda’s Questions

### Buddha Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, have you seen the Dhamma?” “Throughout their lives, your majesty, disciples should proceed with the Buddha as their guide, the Buddha as the one showing the way.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Seeing the Dhamma fourth

## mil3.5.5

5. Question on Reconnecting and Not Transmigrating

### Milinda’s Questions

### Buddha Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, is it so that one does not transmigrate and yet one reconnects?” “Yes, your majesty, one does not transmigrate and yet one reconnects.” “How, Venerable Nāgasena, is it that one does not transmigrate and yet one reconnects? Please give me an analogy.” “Just as, your majesty, if a person kindled one lamp from another would that lamp have transmigrated from the other lamp?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “In the same way, your majesty, one does not transmigrate and yet one reconnects.”

“Give me another analogy.” “Do you remember, your majesty, learning some verse from your teacher of verses?” “Yes, venerable sir.” “Then, your majesty, has that verse transmigrated from the teacher?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “In the same way, your majesty, one does not transmigrate and yet one reconnects.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Reconnecting and Not Transmigrating fifth

## mil3.5.6

6. Question on the Experiencer

### Milinda’s Questions

### Buddha Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, is an experiencer to be found?” The elder responded: “According to ultimate reality, an experiencer is not to be found.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Experiencer sixth

## mil3.5.7

7. Question on Transmigrating to Another Body

### Milinda’s Questions

### Buddha Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, is there any being at all that transmigrates from this body to another body?” “Certainly not, your majesty.” “If, venerable sir, there is no transmigrating from this body to another body, is it not then that one would be freed from evil actions?” “Yes, your majesty, if it would not reconnect, then one would be freed from evil actions. But because, your majesty, it does reconnect, one therefore is not freed from evil actions.”

“Please give me an analogy.” “Just as, your majesty, some person or other might steal another person’s mangos, would that man deserve punishment?” “Yes, venerable sir, he would deserve punishment.” “But, your majesty, those mangos that were taken are not the same as those that were planted, why would he deserve punishment?” “Those mangos, venerable sir, arose dependent on the others, therefore he would deserve punishment.” “In the same way, your majesty, with this name-and-form one performs a lovely or an evil action, and by this action another name-and-form reconnects. Therefore, one is not freed from evil actions.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Transmigrating to Another Body seventh

## mil3.5.8

8. Question on the Existence of the Fruits of Actions

### Milinda’s Questions

### Buddha Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, for a wholesome or unwholesome action done by this name-and-form, where do those actions remain?” “Those actions would follow one, your majesty, like a shadow that never leaves.” “Is it then possible, venerable sir, to point to those actions and say they remain here or there?” “It is not possible, your majesty, to point to those actions and say they remain here or there.”

“Please give me an analogy.” “What do you think, your majesty, for those trees where the fruit has not yet arisen, is it possible to point to those fruits and say those fruits remain here or there?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “In the same way, your majesty, as long as the continuity of life is not cut off, it is not possible to point to those actions and say they remain here or there.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Existence of the Fruits of Actions eighth

## mil3.5.9

9. Question on Knowing Re-arising

### Milinda’s Questions

### Buddha Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, does one who re-arises know, ‘I will re-arise’?” “Yes, your majesty, one who re-arises knows, ‘I will re-arise’.”

“Please give me an analogy.” “Just as, your majesty, when a householder farmer plants seeds in the ground and it rains well, does he know that grain will grow?” “Yes, venerable sir, he would know.” “In the same way, your majesty, one who re-arises knows, ‘I will re-arise’.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Knowing Re-arising ninth

## mil3.6.1

1. Love of the Body Question

### Milinda’s Questions

### Mindfulness Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, is the body dear to recluses?” “No, your majesty, the body is not dear to recluses.” “Then, venerable sir, why do you cherish and treasure it?” “But, your majesty, when you have gone into battle have you been struck by arrows from time to time?” “Yes, venerable sir, I have.” “And would the wound, your majesty, be anointed with a salve, smeared with oil, and wrapped in a soft bandage?” “Yes, venerable sir, the wound would be.” “And was the wound dear to you, your majesty, that it was anointed with a salve, smeared with oil, and wrapped in a soft bandage?” “No, venerable sir, the wound was not dear to me, it was for the healing of the flesh that it was anointed with a salve, smeared with oil, and wrapped in a soft bandage.” “In the same way, your majesty, the body is not dear to recluses, but recluses look after the body without attachment for the maintenance of the holy life. And moreover, your majesty, the body is said by the Blessed One to be like a wound, and therefore recluses look after the body as they would a wound without attachment. And this too was said, your majesty, by the Blessed One:

‘Covered with moist skin, the great wound with nine openings Oozes everywhere putrid-smelling secretions.’”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Love of the Body Question first

## mil3.6.10

10. Question on Remembrance of What was Done Long Ago

### Milinda’s Questions

### Mindfulness Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, how does one remember what was done long ago in the past?” “With mindfulness, your majesty.” “Is it not, Venerable Nāgasena, that one remembers with one’s mind and not with mindfulness?” “Do you not know personally, your majesty, about some task you have done and have forgotten?” “Yes, venerable sir.” “Then were you, your majesty, on that occasion without a mind?” “Certainly not, venerable sir, on that occasion there was no mindfulness.” “So, why do you say, your majesty, that one remembers with one’s mind and not with mindfulness?”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Remembrance of What was Done Long Ago tenth

## mil3.6.11

11. Question on Directly Knowing

### Milinda’s Questions

### Mindfulness Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, does all mindfulness arise through knowing directly or does it come with outside help?” “By knowing directly, your majesty, and with outside help.” “If thus, Venerable Nāgasena, all mindfulness is from directly knowing, there is none with outside help.” “If there were no mindfulness with outside help, your majesty, then artisans would have no need for practice, or craftsmanship, or schooling, and teachers would be useless, but since there is mindfulness with outside help, the opposite is true.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Directly Knowing eleventh

Mindfulness Chapter sixth

In this chapter eleven questions

## mil3.6.2

2. All-knowing Nature Question

### Milinda’s Questions

### Mindfulness Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, is the Buddha all-knowing and all-seeing?” “Yes, your majesty, the Buddha is all-knowing and all-seeing.” “Then why, Venerable Nāgasena, did he proclaim the training steps for his disciples gradually?” “But, your majesty, is there some doctor who knows all the medicines on this earth?” “Yes, venerable sir, there is.” “And would that doctor, your majesty, administer medicine to a sick person at the time of being ill or when not ill?” “He would administer medicine to a sick person, venerable sir, at the time of being ill and not when not ill.” “In the same way, your majesty, the Blessed One who is all-knowing and all-seeing did not proclaim a training rule to his disciples at the wrong time, but when the time was right he proclaimed a training rule to his disciples, which is not to be transgressed all life long.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

All-knowing Nature Question second

## mil3.6.3

3. Characteristics of a Great Person Question

### Milinda’s Questions

### Mindfulness Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, was the Buddha endowed with the thirty-two characteristics of a great person and distinguished with the eighty secondary features, with radiant complexion, skin like gold, and a halo that extends for one fathom?” “Yes, your majesty, he was.”

“But, venerable sir, were his mother and father endowed with the thirty-two characteristics of a great person … and a halo that extends for one fathom?” “No, your majesty, they were not.”

“That being so, venerable sir, is it not then that the Buddha arose endowed with the thirty-two characteristics of a great person … and a halo that extends for one fathom? But surely a child is like their mother or those on their mother’s side or like their father or those on their father’s side.” The elder said, “But is there, your majesty, any lotus with a hundred leaves?” “Yes, venerable sir, there is.” “And where then is the origin for that?” “It is born from mud and it thrives in water.” “Then, your majesty, is the lotus like the mud in colour, smell, or taste?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “Or is it like the water in smell or taste?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “In the same way, your majesty, the Buddha was endowed with the thirty-two characteristics of a great person and distinguished with the eighty secondary features, with radiant complexion, skin like gold, and a halo that extends for one fathom, and his parents were not.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Characteristics of a Great Person Question third

## mil3.6.4

4. Celibacy of the Blessed One Question

### Milinda’s Questions

### Mindfulness Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, was the Buddha a Brahma-farer (celibate)?” “Yes, your majesty, the Buddha was celibate.” “Well then, Venerable Nāgasena, was the Buddha a student of Brahmā?” “But, your majesty, do you have a chief elephant?” “Yes, venerable sir, I do.” “And does this elephant, your majesty, roar the ‘Heron’s roar’ from time to time?” “Yes, venerable sir, he does.” “Well then, your majesty, is this elephant a student of the heron?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “Was Brahmā, your majesty, one with intelligence or without?” “With intelligence, venerable sir.” “Well then, your majesty, Brahmā was a student of the Blessed One.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Celibacy of the Blessed One Question fourth

## mil3.6.5

5. Higher Ordination of the Blessed One Question

### Milinda’s Questions

### Mindfulness Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, is the higher ordination good?” “Yes, your majesty, the higher ordination is good.” “But, Venerable Nāgasena, did the Buddha receive the higher ordination or not?” “The Blessed One, your majesty, reached the highest attainment at the foot of the Bodhi tree, together with omniscience. There was no giving of ordination to the Blessed One by others, in the way that the Blessed One proclaimed a training rule to his disciples, which is not to be transgressed all life long.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Higher Ordination of the Blessed One Question fifth

## mil3.6.6

6. Question on Tears Being a Medicine or Not

### Milinda’s Questions

### Mindfulness Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, those who weep on the death of a mother, and those who weep with affection for the Dhamma, for whom of these two weeping are the tears a medicine, and for whom are the tears not a medicine?” “The tears of one, your majesty, are hot and stained with lust, anger, and delusion, and the tears of the other are cool and unstained with rapture and joy. That which is cool, your majesty, is a medicine; that which is hot is not a medicine.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Tears Being a Medicine or Not sixth

## mil3.6.7

7. Question on the Difference Between Being With Attachment and Without Attachment

### Milinda’s Questions

### Mindfulness Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, what is the difference between being with attachment and being without attachment?” “One is sticky, your majesty, and the other is not sticky.” “What is the meaning, venerable sir, of ‘sticky’ and ‘not sticky’?” “One is desirable, your majesty, and the other is not desirable.” “As I see it, venerable sir, both the one with attachment and the one without attachment wish for beautiful hard and soft food, and neither wishes for something bad.” “One who is not without attachment, your majesty, eats their food and experiences both the taste and the attachment to the taste. But the one who is without attachment eats their food and experiences just the taste and not the attachment to the taste.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Difference Between Being With Attachment and Without Attachment seventh

## mil3.6.8

8. Question on the Establishment of Wisdom

### Milinda’s Questions

### Mindfulness Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, where does wisdom reside?” “Nowhere, your majesty.” “Then, Venerable Nāgasena, is there no wisdom?” “Where does the wind reside, your majesty?” “Nowhere, venerable sir.” “Then, your majesty, is there no wind?”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Establishment of Wisdom eighth

## mil3.6.9

9. Question on Cyclic Existence

### Milinda’s Questions

### Mindfulness Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, as to which you said ‘cyclic existence’. What is this cyclic existence?” “Your majesty, that which is born here dies here, and having died here, it arises elsewhere. Then, having been born there it dies there, and having died there, it arises elsewhere. Thus, your majesty, this is cyclic existence.” “Please give me an analogy.” “Just as, your majesty, some person may eat a ripe mango and plant the seed. Then, when it has grown up as a mature mango tree, it would give fruit. And then that man might eat a ripe mango from it and plant the seed and, when it has grown up as a mature mango tree, it too would give fruit. And thus, no end to these mango trees can be discerned. In the same way, your majesty, that which is born here dies here, and having died here, it arises elsewhere. Then, having been born there it dies there, and having died there, it arises elsewhere. Thus, your majesty, this is cyclic existence.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Cyclic Existence ninth

## mil3.7.1

1. Arising of Mindfulness Question

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, in how many ways does mindfulness arise?” “Your majesty, mindfulness arises in seventeen ways.” “In what seventeen ways?” “Mindfulness arises, your majesty, through knowing directly, with outside help, from consciousness of a substantiality, from consciousness of welfare, from consciousness of misfortune, from a common sign, from an uncommon sign, from understanding due to speech, from a distinguishing characteristic, from remembering, from reckoning, from calculation, from bearing in mind, from mental development, from recording in books, from association of ideas, and from experience.

How does mindfulness arise through knowing directly? Just as, your majesty, Venerable Ānanda or the female lay follower Khujjuttarā or those others who remember past births remember a past birth, thus does mindfulness arise through knowing directly.

How does mindfulness arise with outside help? If others urge one who is naturally muddled in mindfulness so that he remembers, thus does mindfulness arise with outside help.

How does mindfulness arise from consciousness of a substantiality? When there is a consecration into sovereignty or the attainment of the fruit of stream entry, thus does mindfulness arise from consciousness of a substantiality.

How does mindfulness arise from consciousness of welfare? One remembers when one was happy, ‘There was happiness because of that.’ Thus, does mindfulness arise from consciousness of welfare.

How does mindfulness arise from consciousness of misfortune? One remembers when one was suffering, ‘There was suffering because of that.’ Thus, does mindfulness arise from consciousness of misfortune.

How does mindfulness arise from a common sign? Having seen a person similar to oneself one remembers his mother, father, brother, or sister, and having seen a camel, an ox, or a donkey, one remembers a camel, an ox, or a donkey. Thus, does mindfulness arise from a common sign.

How does mindfulness arise from an uncommon sign? When one remembers that such is the colour of a certain thing, such the sound, such the smell, such the taste, such the touch, thus does mindfulness arise from an uncommon sign.

How does mindfulness arise from understanding due to speech? If others remind one who is naturally muddled in mindfulness so that he remembers, thus does mindfulness arise from understanding due to speech.

How does mindfulness arise from a distinguishing characteristic? One who knows an ox based on a conventional factor, remembers it by its distinguishing characteristic. Thus, does mindfulness arise from a distinguishing characteristic.

How does mindfulness arise from remembering? If one causes someone who is usually muddled in mindfulness to remember by repeatedly saying, ‘Remember, sir! Remember, sir!’ thus does mindfulness arise from remembering.

How does mindfulness arise from reckoning? From being trained in writing, one knows, ‘This letter is to be made immediately after that letter.’ Thus, does mindfulness arise from reckoning.

How does mindfulness arise from calculation? From being trained in calculation, accountants calculate even very large sums. Thus, does mindfulness arise from calculation.

How does mindfulness arise from bearing in mind? From being trained in bearing in mind, those who bear in mind can remember even a large amount. Thus, does mindfulness arise from from bearing in mind.

How does mindfulness arise from mental development? Here, a bhikkhu recalls his various past lives, that is, one birth, two births, … such that he remembers them with all their aspects and features. Thus, does mindfulness arise from mental development.

How does mindfulness arise from recording in books? A king’s minister when not remembering an administrative rule might have a book brought to him, and then would remember by means of that book. Thus, does mindfulness arise from from recording in books.

How does mindfulness arise from association of ideas? When one remembers after seeing goods laid aside, thus does mindfulness arise from association of ideas.

How does mindfulness arise from experience? From what was seen one remembers material form, from what was heard one remembers sound, from what was smelled one remembers odour, from what was tasted one remembers flavour, from what was touched one remembers tangibles, from what was cognised one remembers thought. Thus, does mindfulness arise from experience. In these seventeen ways, your majesty, mindfulness arises.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Arising of Mindfulness Question first

## mil3.7.10

10. Question on Long Bones

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, you say thus, ‘There are long bones extending one hundred yojanas.’ Since there is no tree extending that far, how then could there be long bones extending one hundred yojanas?”

“What do you think, your majesty, have you heard that in the great ocean there are fish extending for five hundred yojanas?” “Yes, venerable sir, I have heard so.” “Is it not, your majesty, that the long bones of such a fish would extend for a hundred yojanas?”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Long Bones tenth

## mil3.7.11

11. Question on the Cessation of In and Out Breathing

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king asked, “Venerable Nāgasena, do you say thus, ‘It is possible to cease in and out breathing’?” “Yes, your majesty, it is possible to cease in and out breathing.” “How, Venerable Nāgasena, is it possible to cease in and out breathing?” “What do you think, your majesty, have you heard before of someone snoring?” “Yes, venerable sir, I have heard of it.” “Would that sound be stopped, your majesty, if the body were bent?” “Yes, venerable sir, it would be.” “Since, your majesty, that sound would be stopped if the body were bent by someone undeveloped in body, virtue, mind, and wisdom, why could not the breathing in and the breathing out be ceased for someone developed in body, virtue, mind, and wisdom, and who has attained the fourth jhāna?”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Cessation of In and Out Breathing eleventh

## mil3.7.12

12. Question on the Sea

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, it is called ‘the sea, the sea’. Why is the water called the sea?” The elder replied, “Just as much as there is water, your majesty, so much there is salt. Just as much as there is salt, so much there is water. Therefore, it is called the ‘sea’.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Sea twelfth

## mil3.7.13

13. Question on the One Taste of the Sea

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, why does the sea have just one taste, the taste of salt?” “The sea has just one taste, the taste of salt, your majesty, because it has been around a long time.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the One Taste of the Sea thirteenth

## mil3.7.14

14. Question on the Subtle

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, is it possible to cut through the subtlest of all things?” “Yes, your majesty, it is possible to cut through the subtlest of all things.” “Then what, venerable sir, is the subtlest of all things?” “The Dhamma, your majesty, is the subtlest of all things. But not all qualities are subtle, your majesty, subtle or coarse is a designation for a quality. Whatever is to be cut through, all that is cut through by wisdom. There is nothing else other than wisdom for cutting through.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Subtle fourteenth

## mil3.7.15

15. Question on the Distinction of Consciousness

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king asked, “Venerable Nāgasena, when it is said ‘consciousness’ or ‘wisdom’ or ‘the life principle in a being’, are these things distinct from each other in actuality, or only in name?” “Discrimination, your majesty, is a distinguishing characteristic of consciousness, discernment is a distinguishing characteristic of wisdom, and the life principle in a being cannot be found.” “If the life principle in a being cannot be found, then how is it that one sees a form with the eyes, hears a sound with the ears, smells an odour with the nose, tastes a flavour with the tongue, touches a tangible with the body, and knows a thought with the mind?” The elder said, “If the life principle sees form with the eyes, … and knows thought with the mind, then when the doors of the eyes are destroyed would not the life principle see form more clearly out there before it in the great space? When the ears, the nose, the tongue and the body are all destroyed, would it not hear a sound, smell an odour, taste a flavour, touch a tangible more clearly in the great space?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “Well then, your majesty, the life principle in a being cannot be found.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Distinction of Consciousness fifteenth

## mil3.7.16

16. The Question on the Difficulty of Distinguishing Formless Dhammas

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, did the Blessed One accomplish something difficult?” The elder said, “The Blessed One accomplished something difficult, your majesty.” “But what difficult thing, Venerable Nāgasena, did the Blessed One accomplish?” “The Blessed One accomplished something difficult, your majesty, in that he explained the distinction of these formless mental dhammas that occur with a single object, declaring: ‘This is contact, this is feeling, this is perception, this is volition, this is consciousness.’”

“Please give me an analogy.” “Suppose, your majesty, some man were to go out onto the great ocean in a boat, take up water in the hollow of his hand, taste it with his tongue—would that man be able to know: ‘This is water from the Ganges, this is water from the Yamunā, this is water from the Aciravatī, this is water from the Sarabhū, this is water from the Mahī’?” “It would be difficult to know, venerable sir.” “More difficult than that, your majesty, is what the Blessed One accomplished in explaining the distinction of these formless phenomena that occur with a single object, declaring: ‘This is contact, this is feeling, this is perception, this is volition, this is consciousness.’” “Very good, venerable sir,” said the king in approval.

The Question on the Difficulty of Distinguishing Formless Dhammas sixteenth

Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter seventh

In this chapter sixteen questions

## mil3.7.2

2. Question on Obtaining Mindfulness on a Quality of the Buddha

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king said: “Venerable Nāgasena, you said this, ‘One could do evil for a hundred years, but at the time of death might obtain a single mindful moment on a quality of the Buddha, and thus arise in the realm of the devas,’ I don’t believe this. And then you said, ‘One might arise in a hell realm by killing one person,’ this too I don’t believe.”

“What do you think, your majesty, could even a small stone float on the water without a boat?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “But, your majesty, could even a load of a hundred stones loaded onto a boat float on the water?” “Yes, venerable sir.” “Your majesty, skilled deeds should be considered as like a boat.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Obtaining Mindfulness on a Quality of the Buddha second

## mil3.7.3

3. Question on Striving for the Abandonment of Suffering

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, are you striving for the abandonment of past suffering?” “Certainly not, your majesty.” “Then, venerable sir, are you striving for the abandonment of future suffering?” “Certainly not, your majesty.” “Then, venerable sir, are you striving for the abandonment of present suffering?” “Certainly not, your majesty.” “If you are not striving for the abandonment of past suffering, nor for the abandonment of future suffering, nor for the abandonment of present suffering, then what are you striving for?” The elder said, “Your majesty, I am striving so that this suffering would cease and that no other suffering would arise.”

“Is there now, Venerable Nāgasena, future suffering for you?” “There is not, your majesty.” “Indeed, Venerable Nāgasena, you are very wise that you strive for abandoning future suffering that is not existing.” “Are there, your majesty, any opposing kings, that are adversaries, that have risen up against you?” “Yes, venerable sir, there are.” “Is it then, your majesty, that you had a moat dug, a rampart put up, and a gateway and watchtower constructed?” “Certainly not, venerable sir, these were prepared previously.” “Is it then, your majesty, that you would train in elephant warfare, in horse warfare, in chariot warfare, in archery, and in swordsmanship?” “Certainly not, venerable sir, I had trained in these previously.” “For what reason?” “In order to prevent future perils, venerable sir.” “Is there a future peril now, your majesty?” “There is not, venerable sir.” “Indeed, your majesty, you are very wise that you would prepare for the prevention of a future peril that is not existing.”

“Give me another analogy.” “What do you think, your majesty, when you might become thirsty is it then that you would have a well, a pond, or a reservoir dug, thinking, ‘I will drink water’?” “Certainly not, venerable sir, this has been arranged for previously.” “For what reason?” “It has been arranged, venerable sir, in order to prevent future thirst.” “Is there a future thirst now, your majesty?” “There is not, venerable sir.” “Indeed, your majesty, you are very wise that you would prepare for the prevention of a future thirst that is not existing.”

“Give me another analogy.” “What do you think, your majesty, when you might become hungry is it then that you would have a field ploughed, and rice sowed, thinking, ‘I will eat food’?” “Certainly not, venerable sir, this has been arranged for previously.” “For what reason?” “In order, venerable sir, to prevent future hunger.” “Is there a future hunger now, your majesty?” “There is not, venerable sir.” “Indeed, your majesty, you are very wise that you would prepare for the prevention of a future hunger that is not existing.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Striving for the Abandonment of Suffering third

## mil3.7.4

4. Question on the Brahma World

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king asked, “Venerable Nāgasena, how far away is it from here to the Brahma world?” “So far, your majesty, that if a rock the size of a peaked-roof building had fallen from there, and falling at the speed of eighty-four thousand yojanas per day, it would take four months to land on the earth.”

“Venerable sir, you say thus, ‘Just as a strong man might stretch out his bent arm or bend his stretched arm, in the same way a bhikkhu with psychic powers who has attained mastery over his mind could disappear from Jambudīpa and appear in the Brahma world.’ This I do not believe that one might go so quickly over so many hundreds of yojanas.”

The elder said, “Where then, your majesty, is the place of your birth?” “There is, venerable sir, an island called Alasanda, there I was born.” “How far away, your majesty, is Alasanda from here?” “About two hundred yojanas, venerable sir.” “Do you not know, your majesty, of some business that you have completed there and now remember?” “Yes, venerable sir, I remember.” “Indeed, so quickly you have gone about two hundred yojanas, your majesty!”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Brahma World fourth

## mil3.7.5

5. Question on Arising in Two Places Taking the Same Time

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king said, “Venerable Nāgasena, for one who having passed away is reborn in the Brahma world, and for one who having passed away is reborn in Kashmir, which one takes the longer time, and which the shorter?” “They are the same, your majesty.”

“Give me an analogy.” “Where then, your majesty, is your town of birth?” “There is a place called Kalasigāma, there I was born.” “How far away, your majesty, is Kalasigāma from here?” “About two hundred yojanas, venerable sir.” “How far away, your majesty, is Kashmir from here?” “Twelve yojanas, venerable sir.” “Go on then, your majesty, think about Kalasigāma.” “I have thought about it, venerable sir.” “Go on then, your majesty, think about Kashmir.” “I have thought about it, venerable sir.” “Which thinking took a long time, your majesty, and which a short time?” “They are the same, your majesty.” “Just so, your majesty, for one who having passed away is reborn in the Brahma world, and for one who having passed away is reborn in Kashmir, they happen in the same time.”

“Give me another analogy.” “What do you think, your majesty, if two birds fly in the sky and one sits in a high tree, and the other in a low tree, if these happen at the same time, the shadow of which one would settle on the ground first, and which one later?” “At the same time, venerable sir.” “Just so, your majesty, for one who having passed away is reborn in the Brahma world, and for one who having passed away is reborn in Kashmir, they happen in the same time.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Arising in Two Places Taking the Same Time fifth

## mil3.7.6

6. Question on the Factors for Awakening

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king asked, “Venerable Nāgasena, how many factors for awakening are there?” “There are seven factors for awakening, your majesty.” “By how many of these seven factors for awakening, venerable sir, does one awaken?” “One awakens by one of these, your majesty, the awakening factor of discrimination of states.” “Then, venerable sir, why is it that one speaks of seven factors?” “What do you think, your majesty, if a sword is placed in its sheath and not grasped in the hand is it able to cut what is to be cut?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “In the same way, your majesty, without the investigation of states awakening factor, one cannot awaken by the other six factors.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Factors for Awakening sixth

## mil3.7.7

7. Question on the Increase or Decrease of Merit and Demerit

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king said, “Venerable Nāgasena, which of the two is greater, merit or demerit?” “Merit is greater, your majesty, demerit is small.” “For what reason?” “For one doing demerit, your majesty, there is the regret, ‘I have committed an evil deed’, and therefore demerit does not increase. But for one doing merit, your majesty, there is no regret; and for one without regret gladness arises; for one who is glad joy arises; for one with a joyous mind the body calms down; one with a calmed body experiences happiness; the mind of one who is happy becomes concentrated; and one who is concentrated knows things as they really are. For this reason, merit increases. Even if a man with his hands and feet cut off gives just a handful of lotuses to the Blessed One, he will not go to a hell realm for ninety-one aeons. For this reason, your majesty, merit is greater and demerit is small.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on the Increase or Decrease of Merit and Demerit seventh

## mil3.7.8

8. Question on Doing Evil Knowingly or Unknowingly

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, for whom is the greater demerit, one who knowingly does evil, or one who does evil unknowingly?” The elder replied: “Indeed, your majesty, for him who does evil unknowingly is the greater demerit.” “In that case, Venerable Nāgasena, that one who is our prince or king’s chief minister who unknowingly does evil, we should doubly punish him?” “What do you think, your majesty, who would get burned more, one who knowingly picks up a hot iron ball, ablaze and glowing, or one who unknowingly picks it up?” “Indeed, venerable sir, he who unknowingly picks it up would get burned more.” “Indeed, your majesty, in the same way the greater demerit is for him who does evil unknowingly.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Doing Evil Knowingly or Unknowingly eighth

## mil3.7.9

9. Question on Travelling to Uttarakuru and Other Places

### Milinda’s Questions

### Analysis of Formless Dhammas Chapter

The king asked: “Venerable Nāgasena, is there anybody who, with this mortal body, could go to Uttarakuru or to the Brahma world or to another continent?” “There is, your majesty, someone who could, with this body composed of the four great elements, go to Uttarakuru or to the Brahma world or to another continent.”

“How, Venerable Nāgasena, could one, with this body composed of the four great elements, go to Uttarakuru or to the Brahma world or to another continent?” “Your majesty, have you had the direct experience of jumping a hand span or a cubit on this earth?” “Yes, venerable sir, I have had that direct experience, in fact I jump even eight cubits.” “How, your majesty, do you jump even eight cubits?” “Venerable sir, I generate the thought ‘I will land here,’ and with that thought my body is buoyant.” “In the same way, your majesty, a bhikkhu with psychic power and who has achieved mastery over his own mind, having entrusted his body to his mind, travels in the sky by means of this mind mastery.”

“You are clever, Venerable Nāgasena!”

Question on Travelling to Uttarakuru and Other Places ninth

## mil5.1.1

1. Question on the Fruitfulness of Offerings Made

### Milinda’s Questions

### Questions on Dilemmas

### Supernatural Powers Chapter

Then King Milinda, having been given leave, bowed down at the venerable one’s feet and placing his joined palms in salutation, said this: “Venerable Nāgasena, these sectarians say thus: ‘If the Buddha accepts homage, then the Buddha has not attained final nibbāna, is fettered to the world, existing in the world, sharing in the world—therefore an offering made to him is not in vain but fruitful. If he has attained final nibbāna, is disconnected from the world, departed from all existences, then worship does not reach him. One who has attained final nibbāna accepts nothing, and an offering made to one who accepts nothing is in vain and fruitless.’ This is a two-horned dilemma; it is not within the scope of those of limited understanding—this is only within the scope of the great ones. Break through this net of wrong views and set it on one side. This question is put to you; give insight to the future sons of the Conqueror for the refutation of opposing doctrines.”

The elder said: “The Blessed One has attained final nibbāna, your majesty, and the Blessed One does not accept homage. Acceptance was abandoned at the very foot of the Bodhi tree—how much more so for one who has attained final nibbāna by the element of nibbāna without residue remaining! And this too was said, your majesty, by the elder Sāriputta, the general of the Dhamma:

‘The incomparable ones are venerated by gods and humans. They do not accept honour; this is the nature of the enlightened ones.’”

The king said: “Venerable Nāgasena, when a son speaks praise of his father, or a father speaks praise of his son, this is not a reason for the refutation of opposing doctrines—this is merely a declaration of devotion. Come now, tell me the true reason for this, for establishing your own doctrine and for disentangling the net of wrong views.”

The elder said: “The Blessed One has attained final nibbāna, your majesty, and the Blessed One does not accept worship. Yet, gods and humans, making a jewel out of the relics of the Tathāgata even though he does not accept it, and taking the jewel of the Tathāgata’s knowledge as their focus, and practicing right conduct, they obtain three kinds of success:

Suppose, your majesty, a great mighty mass of fire were to blaze and then be extinguished—does that great mass of fire, your majesty, accept grass and wood as fuel?” “Even while burning, venerable sir, that great mass of fire did not accept grass and wood as fuel—how much less when extinguished, calmed, and without consciousness would it accept them?” “But when that mass of fire has ceased and been calmed, your majesty, does fire become empty in the world?” “Certainly not, venerable sir. Wood is the basis and fuel for fire. Those people who desire fire produce fire by their own strength, power, and energy, through personal effort, by rubbing wood together, and with that fire they perform the tasks that require fire.” “Then, your majesty, the statement of the sectarians is wrong: ‘An offering made to one who does not accept it is in vain and fruitless.’

Just as, your majesty, a great mighty mass of fire blazed, so too the Blessed One blazed with the Buddha-glory throughout the ten-thousand-fold world system. Just as, your majesty, the great mighty mass of fire, having blazed, was extinguished, so too the Blessed One, having blazed with the Buddha-glory throughout the ten-thousand-fold world system, attained final nibbāna by the element of nibbāna without residue remaining. Just as, your majesty, the extinguished mass of fire does not accept grass and wood as fuel, so too the accepting for the benefit of the world has been abandoned and calmed. Just as, your majesty, when the mass of fire is extinguished and without fuel, people by their own strength, power, and energy, through personal effort, produce fire by rubbing wood together and with that fire perform the tasks that require fire, so too, gods and humans, making a jewel out of the relics of the Tathāgata even though he has attained final nibbāna and does not accept it, and taking the jewel of the Tathāgata’s knowledge as their focus, and practicing right conduct, they obtain three kinds of success. By this reason too, your majesty, even though the Tathāgata has attained final nibbāna and does not accept it, an offering made to him is not in vain but fruitful.

Furthermore, your majesty, listen to another reason why an offering made to the Tathāgata who has attained final nibbāna and does not accept it is not in vain but fruitful. Suppose, your majesty, a great mighty wind were to blow and then cease—does that ceased wind, your majesty, accept being produced again?” “Certainly not, venerable sir, the ceased wind has no inclination or attention towards being produced again.” “Why is that?” “Because that wind-element is without consciousness.” “But does the designation ‘wind,’ your majesty, disappear for that ceased wind?” “Certainly not, venerable sir. Palmyra-leaf fans and other fans are conditions for the arising of wind. Whatever people are afflicted by heat and oppressed by burning fever, they produce it by their own strength, power, and energy, through personal effort, with a palmyra-leaf fan or other fan, and with that wind they extinguish the heat and calm the burning fever.” “Then, your majesty, the statement of the sectarians is wrong: ‘An offering made to one who does not accept it is in vain and fruitless.’

Just as, your majesty, a great mighty wind blew, so too the Blessed One blew throughout the ten-thousand-fold world system with the wind of cool, sweet, peaceful, gentle loving-kindness. Just as, your majesty, the great mighty wind, having blown, ceased, so too the Blessed One, having blown with the wind of cool, sweet, peaceful, gentle loving-kindness, attained final nibbāna by the element of nibbāna without residue remaining. Just as, your majesty, the ceased wind does not accept being produced again, so too the accepting for the benefit of the world has been abandoned and calmed. Just as, your majesty, those people are afflicted by heat and oppressed by burning fever, so too gods and humans are oppressed by the burning fever of the threefold fire. Just as palmyra-leaf fans and other fans are conditions for the production of wind, so too the relic and the jewel of knowledge of the Tathāgata are conditions for obtaining the three kinds of success. Just as people afflicted by heat and oppressed by burning fever produce wind with a palmyra-leaf fan or other fan and extinguish the heat and calm the burning fever, so too gods and humans, even though the Tathāgata has attained final nibbāna and does not accept it, by worshipping the relic and the jewel of knowledge, they produce wholesomeness, and with that wholesomeness they extinguish and calm the burning fever of the threefold fire. By this reason too, your majesty, even though the Tathāgata has attained final nibbāna and does not accept it, an offering made to him is not in vain but fruitful.

Furthermore, your majesty, listen to another reason for the refutation of opposing doctrines. Suppose, your majesty, a man were to strike a drum and produce a sound—when that sound of the drum produced by the man disappears, does it accept being produced again?” “Certainly not, venerable sir. That sound has disappeared; it has no inclination or attention towards being produced again. Once the sound of the drum that was produced has disappeared, that sound is completely cut off. But the drum, venerable sir, is a condition for the production of sound, and then when the condition exists, a man strikes the drum with self-generated effort and produces sound.”

“In the same way, your majesty, the Blessed One, having appointed as the teacher the jewel of the relics and the Dhamma, the Vinaya, and the instruction, and developed virtue, concentration, wisdom, liberation, and the knowledge and vision of liberation, himself attained final nibbāna by the element of nibbāna without residue remaining. Yet with the Blessed One’s attainment of final nibbāna, the obtaining of attainment (by others) is not cut off. Beings oppressed by the suffering of rebirth, having made the jewel of the relics, the Dhamma, the Vinaya, and the instruction their condition, desiring attainment, obtain attainment. By this reason too, your majesty, even though the Tathāgata has attained final nibbāna and does not accept it, an offering made to him is not in vain but fruitful.

And this, your majesty, was foreseen by the Blessed One for future time. It was spoken, said, and declared: ‘It may occur to you, Ānanda, thus: “The teaching has lost its Teacher; we have no Teacher.” But this, Ānanda, should not be seen in that way. Whatever Dhamma and Vinaya that have been taught and laid down by me for you, that will be your Teacher after my passing.’ That statement of those sectarians— ‘An offering made to the Tathāgata who has attained final nibbāna and does not accept it is in vain and fruitless’—that statement of theirs is wrong, untrue, false, lying, contradictory, contrary, productive of suffering, resulting in suffering, leading to lower realms.

Furthermore, your majesty, listen to another reason why an offering made to the Tathāgata who has attained final nibbāna and does not accept it is not in vain but fruitful. Does this great earth, your majesty, accept, saying ‘May all seeds germinate in me’?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “But why then, your majesty, do those seeds, having germinated in the great earth that does not accept them, become established with firm roots and rootlets, spread with trunk, heartwood, and branches, bearing flowers and fruits?” “Even though the great earth is not the one that accepts, venerable sir, it is the basis for those seeds and gives a condition for their germination. Those seeds, depending on that basis and by means of that condition, having germinated, become established with firm roots and rootlets, spread with trunk, heartwood, and branches, bearing flowers and fruits.” “Then, your majesty, the sectarians are lost, defeated, and contradicted in their own doctrine if they say, ‘An offering made to one who does not accept it is in vain and fruitless.’

Just as the great earth is, your majesty, so is the Tathāgata, the Worthy One, the Perfectly Enlightened One. Just as, your majesty, the great earth accepts nothing, so the Tathāgata accepts nothing. Just as, your majesty, those seeds, depending on the earth, having germinated, become established with firm roots and rootlets, spread with trunk, heartwood, and branches, bearing flowers and fruits—so too gods and humans, depending on the relic and the jewel of knowledge of the Tathāgata who has attained final nibbāna and does not accept, become established with firm wholesome roots, spread with the trunk of concentration, the heartwood of Dhamma, and the branches of virtue, bearing the flowers of liberation and the fruits of the recluseship. By this reason too, your majesty, even though the Tathāgata has attained final nibbāna and does not accept it, an offering made to him is not in vain but fruitful.

Furthermore, your majesty, listen to another reason why an offering made to the Tathāgata who has attained final nibbāna and does not accept it is not in vain but fruitful. Do these camels, oxen, donkeys, goats, animals, and humans, your majesty, accept the arising of colonies of worms in their bellies?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “But why then, your majesty, do those worms arise in the bellies of those beings who do not accept them, and through having many offspring reach abundance?” “Through the power of evil kamma, venerable sir, even though those beings do not accept them, worms arise in their bellies and through having many offspring reach abundance.” “In the same way, your majesty, through the strength of the objective support for the (meditation) on the knowledge and the relics of the Tathāgata who has attained final nibbāna and does not accept, an offering made to the Tathāgata is not in vain but fruitful.

Furthermore, your majesty, listen to another reason why an offering made to the Tathāgata who has attained final nibbāna and does not accept it is not in vain but fruitful. Do humans, your majesty, accept these ninety-eight diseases arising in the body?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “But why then, your majesty, do those diseases fall upon the bodies of those who do not accept them?” “Through evil conduct done previously, venerable sir.” “If, your majesty, unwholesome action done previously is to be experienced here, then, your majesty, both wholesome and unwholesome kamma done previously or done here is not in vain but fruitful. By this reason too, your majesty, even though the Tathāgata has attained final nibbāna and does not accept, an offering made to him is not in vain but fruitful.

But have you heard before, your majesty, about the yakkha named Nandaka who, having assaulted the elder Sāriputta, entered into the earth?” “Yes, venerable sir, I have heard it; this is well-known in the world.” “But did the elder Sāriputta, your majesty, accept the great earth swallowing the yakkha Nandaka?” “Even if the world with its gods were torn apart, venerable sir, even if the sun and moon were to fall to the ground, even if Mount Sineru, king of mountains, were to be crumbling away, the elder Sāriputta would not accept another’s suffering. Why is that? Whatever cause by which the elder Sāriputta might become angry or hostile—that cause has been eradicated and cut off by the elder Sāriputta. Through the complete eradication of the cause, venerable sir, the elder Sāriputta would not show anger even to one taking his life.” “If, your majesty, the elder Sāriputta did not accept the earth swallowing the yakkha Nandaka, why then did the yakkha Nandaka enter into the earth?” “Through the power of unwholesome kamma, venerable sir.” “If, your majesty, through the power of unwholesome kamma the yakkha Nandaka entered into the earth, then even an offense committed against one who does not accept it is not in vain but fruitful. Therefore, your majesty, through the power of unwholesome kamma too, an offering made to one who does not accept it is not in vain but fruitful. By this reason too, your majesty, even though the Tathāgata has attained final nibbāna and does not accept it, an offering made to him is not in vain but fruitful.

How many people are there, your majesty, who in the present time have entered into the great earth? Have you heard of them?” “Yes, venerable sir, I have heard.” “Come then, your majesty, tell me.” “The brahmin girl Ciñca, venerable sir, Suppabuddha the Sakyan, the elder Devadatta, the yakkha Nandaka, and the brahmin youth Nanda. I have heard, venerable sir, that these five people have entered into the great earth.” “Against whom did they offend, your majesty?” “Against the Blessed One, venerable sir, and against his disciples.” “But did the Blessed One or his disciples, your majesty, accept this entering into the great earth by them?” “Certainly not, venerable sir.” “Then, your majesty, even though the Tathāgata has attained final nibbāna and does not accept it, an offering made to him is not in vain but fruitful.” “Well explained, Venerable Nāgasena! The profound question has been made clear, that which was hidden has been revealed, the knot has been untied, the thicket has been cleared away, the opposing doctrines are destroyed, the false views are broken, the sectarians have become without splendour; you are the chief, the best of those worthy of a teacher’s chair!”

Question on the Fruitfulness of Offerings Made first

## mil5.1.10

5.1.10. The Limit Of Three Months

### The Questions of King Milinda

### Book 4: The solving of dilemmas

### Chapter 1

‘Venerable Nāgasena, it has been said by the Blessed One: “The Tathāgata, Ānanda, has thought out and thoroughly practised, developed, accumulated, and ascended to the very height of the four paths to saintship, and so mastered them as to be able to use them as a means of mental advancement, and as a basis for edification—and he therefore, Ānanda, should he desire it, might remain alive for a Kalpa, or for that portion of a Kalpa which has yet to run.” And again he said: “At the end of three months from this time the Tathāgata will die.” If the first of these statements were true, then the limit of three months must have been false. If the second were true, Then the first must have been false. For the Tathāgatas boast not without an occasion, the Blessed Buddhas speak no misleading words, but they utter truth, and speak sincerely. This too is a double-headed dilemma, profound, subtle, hard to expound. It is now put to you. Tear in sunder this net of heresy, put it on one side, break in pieces the arguments of the adversary!’

‘Both these statements, O king, were made by the Blessed One. But Kalpa in that connection means the duration of a man’s life. And the Blessed One, O king, was not exalting his own power when he said so, but he was exalting the power of Saintship. It was as if a king were possessed of a horse most swift of foot, who could run like the wind. And in order to exalt the power of his speed the king were to say in the presence of all his court-townsfolk and country folk, hired servants and men of war, brahmins, nobles, and officers: “If he wished it this noble steed of mine could cross the earth to its ocean boundary, and be back here again, in a moment!” Now though he did not try to test the horse’s speed in the presence of the court, yet it had that speed, and was, really able to go along over the earth to its ocean boundary in a moment. Just so, O king, the Blessed One spake as he did in praise of the power of saintship, and so spake seated in the midst of gods and men, and of the men of the threefold wisdom and the sixfold insight—the Arahats pure and free from stain—when he said: “The Tathāgata, Ānanda, has thought out and practised, developed, accumulated, and ascended to the very height of the four powers of saintship, and so mastered them as to be able to use them as a means of mental advancement, as a basis for edification. And he therefore, Ānanda, should he desire it, might remain alive for a Kalpa, or the part of a Kalpa that has yet to run.” And there was that power, O king, in the Tathāgata, he could have remained alive for that time: and yet he did not show that power in the midst of that assembly. The Blessed One, O king, is free from desire as respects all conditions of future life, and has condemned them all. For it has been said, O king, by the Blessed One: “Just, O Bhikkhus, as a very small quantity of excrement is of evil smell, so do I find no beauty in the very smallest degree of future life, not even in such for the time of the snapping of the fingers.” Now would the Blessed One, O king, who thus looked upon all sorts and conditions of future life as dung have nevertheless, simply because of his power of Iddhi, harboured a craving desire for future life?’

‘Certainly not, Sir.’

‘Then it must have been to exalt the power of Iddhi that he gave utterance to such a boast.’

‘Very good, Nāgasena! It is so, and I accept it as you say.

Here ends the dilemma as to the three months.

Here ends the First Chapter.
